tbmm-2021-02-18sit01spe0105par0411	Thank you, Mr. President. Mr. President, dear lawmakers, I salute you all. We propose that a research commission be established for the reasons for hate speech, discrimination and hate crimes, for the purposes of the necessary efforts to fight legal and democratic warfare against them, to prevent hatred in society and to prepare for the law of hate. The hate crime is described as the crime the perpetrator developed against another person who he knew or assumed had one of those qualities, with a prejudice about religion, language, race, ethnicity, disability, gender and sexual orientation. There are also those who describe a person's involvement in a minority group as being subjected to harmful or insulting behavior or words. In addition to crimes against prejudicial motivation, murder, rape and property, the threat of violence and all other humiliating actions are also considered a hate crime. The hate crime is not in any legal text, not primarily in our legislation. For the first time, the concept of hate crime began to be conceptually used, especially in the late 1980s, when a white group in the United States carried out widespread attacks on blacks and were involved in the media. Two conditions are said to exist in order to be mentioned as a hate crime: First, a hate crime must be made clear in the penal code, and second, the perpetrator must have committed this crime with a hatred or prejudice due to the individual's belonging to a certain group. I told you in our criminal code, it's a hate crime. Not clearly edited, but the 10th article of the Constitution clearly states that everyone is equal to the law, regardless of language, race, color, gender, political thought, philosophy, religion, religion, and so on. In Article 3 of the Turkish Penal Code, the principle of justice and equality in front of the law is clearly arranged, but the Constitution and the Turkish Penal Code are not considered by the courts. Of course, it is an important topic of discussion that the crime of hate is not clearly or covert in the penal code, but international contracts on fundamental rights and freedoms can be referred to in this regard, and international contracts can be used to ensure the punishment of hate crimes. Today, the main reason why we have submitted this motion is not because the penal code has been set up; our main goal is the way to hate crime, the speech of hate. The prevalence of hate speech is also a way to prevent a real hate crime from being discussed in a country. Look, I'll give you a couple of examples: the LGBTIs are called "the perverts" who run this country. When we talk about our Armenian citizens, excuse me, Armenian. The main opposition party leader's sect can, in some way, be booed in the squares, or called the opposition party, for our party, the pests that need to be described, can be called the pests. Now, that's all hate speech. If those who rule the country, leaders of political parties, those who make promises on behalf of their political parties, start using these hate speech in a normal way, it can be seen as common words in the broadest parts of this community. So, if we set up a parliamentary research commission, this commission is composing these hate statements. He can examine it somehow and at least create a substructure that will keep people from saying that in large parts of society. Maybe this commission's preparation could also be the basis for the arrangement of hate crimes in the future. First things first, first things first, if we pay attention to what we say, maybe we can stop hate crimes from happening. If a person who speaks for power uses those words of hate...
